So yeah… it’s causing distress, and it’s definitely getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions over and over, like touching or checking your face, in an attempt to make it look better or to reassure yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I don’t like admitting it, but yes. It’s become this whole routine, especially before I leave for work or if I’m supposed to go somewhere like my sister’s game night.\n\nI’ll be in the bathroom and I’ll check it in the mirror, then I’ll turn my face a little, then I’ll flip on a different light or lean closer, then back up, because somehow it looks different depending on where I’m standing. And I’ll keep thinking, “Okay, maybe it’s not that bad,” but then I catch it from another angle and it looks rough again, so I start over. Sometimes I rub at the spot, like maybe if I smooth it or wipe it enough it’ll look less noticeable, even though I know that doesn’t really make sense.\n\nAnd if I’m still feeling like it looks obvious, I’ll put on a hat and pull it kind of low, like that’s going to help hide that side of my face. Or I’ll just start thinking of excuses not to go. Like with my sister’s game night, I’ll tell myself I’m tired or I’ve got something to do early, but really it’s because I stood there looking at my face too long and convinced myself everyone’s going to see it.\n\nSo yeah, it’s checking, turning, rubbing, trying to cover it… and then checking again to see if any of that helped. It can eat up a lot more time than I mean for it to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry that something is wrong with your face beyond just its appearance, like it's dirty or contaminated in some way?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not like dirty or contaminated, if that’s what you mean. I mean, I work construction, so there are definitely days where I come home with dust or grime on me, and I’ll wash my face like anyone would. But this thing I’m worried about doesn’t feel like dirt, exactly.\n\nIt’s more like… when I look at it, it looks damaged or rough to me. Like the skin there is wrong-looking, not unclean. I don’t usually think, “Oh, there’s something on my face I need to scrub off,” except sometimes I rub at it because I’m hoping it’ll look smoother or less noticeable. But I don’t have this fear that it’s contaminated or anything like that.\n\nI guess I just keep thinking it looks bad. Like weathered or sunken or off somehow. But no, not dirty in that way.\n\nDoctor: Is it always this one particular patch on your face that bothers you, or are there other parts of your body that you also worry about?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly that one patch, yeah. I mean, I can get self-conscious like anybody, I guess, but the thing that really grabs me is this rough, kind of sunken-looking area on my face. It’s like one side of me looks older or more beat-up than the rest, if that makes sense.\n\nI notice it in the mirror, but photos are the worst. In a picture, I’ll zoom right to that side of my face and it’s like I can’t unsee it. Other people will say, “I barely notice anything,” or they’ll act like I’m making too much of it, but to me it stands out right away. It’s the reason I’m here, really — I can’t stop seeing it. Not really other body parts, no. It’s this spot on my face.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding social interactions or relationships because of your concerns about your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. More than I want to admit, honestly. It’s not like I’ve cut everybody off or anything, but I definitely avoid things now.\n\nLike with work, if they’re taking site photos or somebody wants a picture of the crew, I’ll kind of drift to the back or make an excuse like I need to grab something from the truck. I know that probably sounds ridiculous, because it’s just a job site and everyone’s dirty and tired anyway, but I start thinking that if the light hits my face wrong, that patch is going to show up really bad in the photo. And once it’s in a picture, I feel like it’s just there forever for people to look at.\n\nAnd getting out the door in the morning has gotten harder too. I’ve been late to construction jobs because I’m stuck in the bathroom checking my face, trying a hat, taking it off, looking again. Then I’m rushing and angry at myself because I know I should’ve left ten minutes ago.\n\nWith family stuff, yeah… I’ve skipped some of my sister’s game nights. I’ll say I’m tired from work, which is partly believable because I am tired, but the real reason is I don’t want to sit around a table with everyone facing me, looking at me under bright lights. I start imagining them noticing that side of my face, even if they don’t say anything. So I just don’t go sometimes. Then I feel bad afterward, because I like seeing them. I just don’t want them looking at me.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about your face focused on this one particular patch, or do you sometimes worry about other aspects of your appearance as well?\n\nPatient: It’s really that one patch, mostly. I mean… I don’t love getting older in general, nobody does, but this isn’t like I’m standing there picking apart every single thing about myself. The thing I can’t get past is that rough, kind of sunken-looking spot on my face.\n\nIt’s like when I look in the mirror, my eye goes right to it. And in photos it’s worse — I’ll look at the picture and it’s like one side of my face looks old, worn down, beat-up, and I can’t see the rest of the picture normally after that. Other people keep telling me they barely notice anything, or they say it’s just lighting or normal skin, but to me it stands out immediately.\n\nSo, yeah, I’m here because I can’t stop seeing that one area. It’s not really my whole body or a bunch of different features. It’s that spot on my face that keeps catching me.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your face affected your ability to perform your job as a construction worker?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. I mean, I still do the work once I’m there — I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t swing a hammer or carry materials because of my face — but it’s affected getting there and how I act on site.\n\nThe biggest thing is the morning. I’ve been late getting out the door for construction jobs because I get stuck checking it. I’ll be in the bathroom looking at that side of my face, then I’ll try a hat, change the angle, look again, maybe check near the window because the light is different there… and before I know it, I’m behind. Then I’m driving in all stressed out and mad at myself because it’s not like the crew cares about my face, they care that I’m there on time.\n\nAnd on the job site, if someone’s taking photos — progress pictures, crew pictures, whatever — I try to dodge them. I’ll step away or act like I need to grab something. I just don’t want that patch showing up in a picture where everyone can see it later. So, yeah, it hasn’t stopped me from doing construction, exactly, but it has definitely gotten in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face, or is it something you can stop doing if you want to?\n\nPatient: It feels like I have to, yeah. I mean, I can tell myself, “Just leave it alone, you’re going to be late,” but then I’ll still go back to the bathroom mirror one more time. And then one more time after that.\n\nBefore work especially, I’ll stand there turning my face different ways, like trying to catch it under the overhead light, then maybe by the window, because somehow I think if I can see exactly how bad it looks, I’ll feel better. But I usually don’t. I’ll rub at the spot too, like maybe it’ll smooth out or look less rough, even though I know that probably doesn’t make sense.\n\nSame thing before going to my sister’s game night. I’ll check it and check it, and then I’ll try wearing a hat low, look again, adjust it, take it off, put it back on. If I decide it looks too obvious that day, sometimes I’ll just make an excuse not to go. So… I guess I can stop eventually, because I do leave the bathroom at some point, but it doesn’t feel like I can just choose to not check it. It kind of pulls me back.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if you could somehow fix or hide this patch on your face, your concerns and behaviors related to it would completely go away?\n\nPatient: I want to say yes, because it feels like everything is about that one spot. Like if that rough, kind of sunken-looking patch just wasn’t there, or if I could cover it so it didn’t show in the mirror or in photos, then I think I’d finally be able to stop staring at it.\n\nBecause that’s really what brings me here — I can’t stop seeing it. I’ll look at my face and it’s like my eye goes straight to that area, like one side of me looks older, worn down, beat-up almost. And then people tell me, “I barely notice it,” or “It’s just the lighting,” but that doesn’t really settle it for me, because I still see it immediately.\n\nSo… yeah, I think if it were truly fixed or hidden, the worry would probably go way down. Maybe completely. It’s hard to imagine because right now it’s like I can’t look at myself normally without that patch jumping out at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control when you're checking your face, or do you feel like you're doing it automatically?\n\nPatient: It’s kind of both, but more like it’s automatic once I get started. Like before work, I’ll tell myself I’m just going to take one quick look in the bathroom mirror, make sure it’s not too bad, and then leave. But then I’m turning my face this way and that way under the light, then I’ll move closer, then farther away, and I’ll check it again like maybe I missed something.\n\nAnd the lighting makes it worse, because I’ll think, “Well, it looks bad under the bathroom light, but what about by the window?” So I go over there and check it in that light too. Then I’ll rub at the spot, like I’m trying to smooth it down or see if it feels as bad as it looks, even though I know standing there doing that isn’t really helping anything.\n\nIt doesn’t feel like I sit there and calmly decide to do all that. It’s more like I get pulled into it. Same thing if I’m going to my sister’s game night — I’ll check it, put a hat on low, look again, adjust the hat, take it off, put it back on, and then sometimes if I think it’s too obvious, I’ll just make an excuse not to go. So yeah… I don’t feel very in control of it. It’s like I’m doing it before I even fully realize how long I’ve been standing there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are judging you because of this patch on your face?\n\nPatient: I guess… yeah, I worry they are. I don’t know if I can prove it, because people tell me they barely notice anything, and maybe they mean that. But when I see it in the mirror or in photos, that rough, kind of sunken-looking patch is the first thing I see. It makes one side of my face look old, like worn down or beat-up, and then I assume other people must be seeing the same thing.\n\nSo if someone looks at me a little too long, or if I’m talking to somebody face-to-face, I start thinking, “They’re looking at that spot.” Even if they don’t say anything. It’s hard to believe they’re not judging it when it looks so obvious to me. That’s pretty much why I’m here, honestly — I can’t stop seeing it, even when everyone else says it’s nothing or they barely notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if nobody else could see this patch on your face, you would still feel the need to check on it and try to fix it?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, I probably still would. I mean, part of me wants to say if nobody else could see it, then maybe I’d relax, because a lot of the panic is about going out and having someone notice it. But even when I’m alone, it’s still there for me. I still see it.\n\nLike before work, even if I’m not really seeing anyone close up right away, I’ll get stuck in the bathroom checking it. I turn my face under the light, then I’ll move a little, check from another angle, sometimes go by a different light because I’m trying to figure out if it looks worse there. And I rub at it, like somehow I’m going to smooth it out or make it look less rough, even though I know that doesn’t really do anything.\n\nSame thing if I’m supposed to go to my sister’s game night. I’ll check it, put a hat on low, look again, adjust it, look again… and if I think that patch is too obvious, I might just make some excuse and not go. So even if nobody else could see it, I think I’d still be checking, because I would know it was there. It’s like I can’t leave it alone once I notice it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your life would be significantly different if you didn't have this concern about the patch on your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it would be different. Pretty noticeably, honestly. I don’t know if everything in my life would magically be perfect, but this one thing eats up way more space than it should.\n\nLike with work, I do construction, so mornings already aren’t exactly relaxed. I need to get out the door, get to the site, be ready to go. But I’ve been late because I get stuck checking my face before I leave. I’ll think I’m just checking once, and then it turns into looking at it under one light, then another, messing with my hat, trying to figure out if it’s as bad as I think it is. And then suddenly I’m behind, and I’m rushing, or I’m showing up later than I should. That’s embarrassing too, because it’s not like I want to tell the guys, “Sorry, I was staring at my face in the mirror again.”\n\nAnd then there are little things that shouldn’t be a big deal, like site photos. If someone’s taking pictures of the crew or progress shots and people are in them, I try to dodge out of the way. I don’t want my face showing up in some picture where everyone can zoom in or where I’m caught at a bad angle. I know that probably sounds excessive, but in the moment it feels like I have to avoid it.\n\nFamily stuff too. My sister has game nights sometimes, and I’ve skipped some of those because I couldn’t get myself to go if I thought the patch looked too obvious that day. I’ll make an excuse, like I’m tired or something came up, but really it’s because I don’t want to sit across from people with them looking at my face all night.\n\nSo yeah… if I didn’t have this concern, I think I’d be more on time, I’d probably show up for things more, and I wouldn’t be spending so much energy trying to hide or avoid being looked at.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would be acceptable to you if you could be certain that nobody else noticed this patch?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… or at least I don’t think I’d be completely okay with it.\n\nI mean, if I could be absolutely sure nobody else noticed, maybe that would take some of the pressure off. I wouldn’t be sitting there thinking every person is staring at it or judging me for it. But the thing is, I still notice it. I’m here because I can’t stop seeing it myself. In the mirror, in photos, it’s like my eye goes straight to that rough, sunken-looking patch, and then the rest of my face kind of disappears.\n\nIt makes one side of my face look old to me, like beat-up or worn down, and even if people keep saying they barely notice anything, I don’t really experience it that way. To me it’s right there. So no, I don’t think my face would suddenly feel acceptable just because nobody else saw it. It might be less embarrassing, maybe, but I’d still be bothered by it because I’d know it was there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would look normal to you if you could somehow completely remove or cover up the patch you're concerned about?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think if it was completely gone, or covered so well that I couldn’t see it at all, then maybe my face would look more normal to me. I think that’s the part I get stuck on — not my whole face exactly, but that one rough, sunken-looking patch. Once I see it, it’s like my eye just locks onto it.\n\nLike in the mirror, I’m not just seeing “my face.” I’m seeing that spot first. Same with photos. Even if it’s a group picture or something where other people probably wouldn’t even look that closely, I zoom right in on that side of my face, and it looks old to me, like worn down or beat-up compared to the other side. And people will say they barely notice anything, or that it just looks like normal skin texture, but that doesn’t really match what I see.\n\nSo if you mean if that exact patch was completely removed or hidden, then yeah, I think I’d probably feel like I looked more normal. But I don’t know… I also worry I’d still be checking to make sure it was really covered or really gone. But the main thing is that patch. That’s what makes my face look wrong to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check and fix your face if you were completely alone and there was no chance of anyone else seeing you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I probably would, at least some. Maybe not in exactly the same frantic way as when I’m about to be around people, but I don’t think it would just disappear because nobody could see me.\n\nLike, before work or before going over to my sister’s for game night, that’s when it gets the worst. I’ll be in the bathroom mirror way too long, turning my face one way and then the other, trying the overhead light, then leaning toward the side where the light hits different, checking if the patch looks deeper or rougher. And I’ll rub at it, even though I know that doesn’t really fix anything. It’s like I’m trying to smooth it out or make it look less obvious, but mostly I’m just making myself more focused on it.\n\nAnd then I start messing with how I’m going to look when I leave — like wearing my hat low, angling it a certain way, seeing if it shadows that side of my face. If I’m going to my sister’s and I think it looks too obvious, sometimes I’ll just come up with an excuse not to go. I’ll tell myself I’m tired or I’ve got something else, but really it’s because I got stuck looking at it and couldn’t stand the idea of walking in like that.\n\nSo if I was completely alone, no one around, no chance of anybody seeing me… I think I’d still check it because I’d still know it was there. But the fixing part, the hat, the trying to make it okay enough to go out — that’s definitely worse when I’m about to see people.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to check and fix your face is more about making yourself feel better about how you look or about avoiding what others might think of your appearance?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, honestly… but if I really slow down and think about it, the checking starts with me trying to feel okay with what I’m seeing. Like I’m trying to get to a point where I can look in the mirror and think, “Alright, it’s not that bad, I can leave the house.” But then it turns into thinking about other people seeing it, too.\n\nBefore work, for example, I’ll be in the bathroom and I’ll keep turning my face under different lights, trying to see if that patch looks rougher from one angle or more sunken from another. I’ll stand there way longer than I mean to. It’s not like I’m calmly grooming myself — it’s more like I’m trying to catch the worst version of it before somebody else does. And I’ll rub at the spot, like maybe I can smooth it out somehow, even though I know that doesn’t really work.\n\nSame thing if I’m supposed to go to my sister’s game night. I’ll check it, then check it again, then try wearing my hat low to see if it hides that side of my face better. And if I think it still looks too obvious, sometimes I just don’t go. I’ll make some excuse, like I’m tired or not feeling up to it, but really it’s because I couldn’t get myself comfortable with how my face looked.\n\nSo… I guess the fixing is partly to calm myself down, but it’s also to avoid walking into a place feeling like people are going to notice. It’s hard to separate the two, because if I don’t feel okay about it, I assume other people won’t either.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. But I usually don’t. I’ll rub at the spot too, like maybe it’ll smooth out or look less rough, even though I know that probably doesn’t make sense.\n\nSame thing before going to my sister’s game night. I’ll check it and check it, and then I’ll try wearing a hat low, look again, adjust it, take it off, put it back on. If I decide it looks too obvious that day, sometimes I’ll just make an excuse not to go. So… I guess I can stop eventually, because I do leave the bathroom at some point, but it doesn’t feel like I can just choose to not check it. It kind of pulls me back.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if you could somehow fix or hide this patch on your face, your concerns and behaviors related to it would completely go away?\n\nPatient: I want to say yes, because it feels like everything is about that one spot. Like if that rough, kind of sunken-looking patch just wasn’t there, or if I could cover it so it didn’t show in the mirror or in photos, then I think I’d finally be able to stop staring at it.\n\nBecause that’s really what brings me here — I can’t stop seeing it. I’ll look at my face and it’s like my eye goes straight to that area, like one side of me looks older, worn down, beat-up almost. And then people tell me, “I barely notice it,” or “It’s just the lighting,” but that doesn’t really settle it for me, because I still see it immediately.\n\nSo… yeah, I think if it were truly fixed or hidden, the worry would probably go way down. Maybe completely. It’s hard to imagine because right now it’s like I can’t look at myself normally without that patch jumping out at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control when you're checking your face, or do you feel like you're doing it automatically?\n\nPatient: It’s kind of both, but more like it’s automatic once I get started. Like before work, I’ll tell myself I’m just going to take one quick look in the bathroom mirror, make sure it’s not too bad, and then leave. But then I’m turning my face this way and that way under the light, then I’ll move closer, then farther away, and I’ll check it again like maybe I missed something.\n\nAnd the lighting makes it worse, because I’ll think, “Well, it looks bad under the bathroom light, but what about by the window?” So I go over there and check it in that light too. Then I’ll rub at the spot, like I’m trying to smooth it down or see if it feels as bad as it looks, even though I know standing there doing that isn’t really helping anything.\n\nIt doesn’t feel like I sit there and calmly decide to do all that. It’s more like I get pulled into it. Same thing if I’m going to my sister’s game night — I’ll check it, put a hat on low, look again, adjust the hat, take it off, put it back on, and then sometimes if I think it’s too obvious, I’ll just make an excuse not to go. So yeah… I don’t feel very in control of it. It’s like I’m doing it before I even fully realize how long I’ve been standing there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are judging you because of this patch on your face?\n\nPatient: I guess… yeah, I worry they are. I don’t know if I can prove it, because people tell me they barely notice anything, and maybe they mean that. But when I see it in the mirror or in photos, that rough, kind of sunken-looking patch is the first thing I see. It makes one side of my face look old, like worn down or beat-up, and then I assume other people must be seeing the same thing.\n\nSo if someone looks at me a little too long, or if I’m talking to somebody face-to-face, I start thinking, “They’re looking at that spot.” Even if they don’t say anything. It’s hard to believe they’re not judging it when it looks so obvious to me. That’s pretty much why I’m here, honestly — I can’t stop seeing it, even when everyone else says it’s nothing or they barely notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if nobody else could see this patch on your face, you would still feel the need to check on it and try to fix it?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, I probably still would. I mean, part of me wants to say if nobody else could see it, then maybe I’d relax, because a lot of the panic is about going out and having someone notice it. But even when I’m alone, it’s still there for me. I still see it.\n\nLike before work, even if I’m not really seeing anyone close up right away, I’ll get stuck in the bathroom checking it. I turn my face under the light, then I’ll move a little, check from another angle, sometimes go by a different light because I’m trying to figure out if it looks worse there. And I rub at it, like somehow I’m going to smooth it out or make it look less rough, even though I know that doesn’t really do anything.\n\nSame thing if I’m supposed to go to my sister’s game night. I’ll check it, put a hat on low, look again, adjust it, look again… and if I think that patch is too obvious, I might just make some excuse and not go. So even if nobody else could see it, I think I’d still be checking, because I would know it was there. It’s like I can’t leave it alone once I notice it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your life would be significantly different if you didn't have this concern about the patch on your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it would be different. Pretty noticeably, honestly. I don’t know if everything in my life would magically be perfect, but this one thing eats up way more space than it should.\n\nLike with work, I do construction, so mornings already aren’t exactly relaxed. I need to get out the door, get to the site, be ready to go. But I’ve been late because I get stuck checking my face before I leave. I’ll think I’m just checking once, and then it turns into looking at it under one light, then another, messing with my hat, trying to figure out if it’s as bad as I think it is. And then suddenly I’m behind, and I’m rushing, or I’m showing up later than I should. That’s embarrassing too, because it’s not like I want to tell the guys, “Sorry, I was staring at my face in the mirror again.”\n\nAnd then there are little things that shouldn’t be a big deal, like site photos. If someone’s taking pictures of the crew or progress shots and people are in them, I try to dodge out of the way. I don’t want my face showing up in some picture where everyone can zoom in or where I’m caught at a bad angle. I know that probably sounds excessive, but in the moment it feels like I have to avoid it.\n\nFamily stuff too. My sister has game nights sometimes, and I’ve skipped some of those because I couldn’t get myself to go if I thought the patch looked too obvious that day. I’ll make an excuse, like I’m tired or something came up, but really it’s because I don’t want to sit across from people with them looking at my face all night.\n\nSo yeah… if I didn’t have this concern, I think I’d be more on time, I’d probably show up for things more, and I wouldn’t be spending so much energy trying to hide or avoid being looked at.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would be acceptable to you if you could be certain that nobody else noticed this patch?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… or at least I don’t think I’d be completely okay with it.\n\nI mean, if I could be absolutely sure nobody else noticed, maybe that would take some of the pressure off. I wouldn’t be sitting there thinking every person is staring at it or judging me for it. But the thing is, I still notice it. I’m here because I can’t stop seeing it myself. In the mirror, in photos, it’s like my eye goes straight to that rough, sunken-looking patch, and then the rest of my face kind of disappears.\n\nIt makes one side of my face look old to me, like beat-up or worn down, and even if people keep saying they barely notice anything, I don’t really experience it that way. To me it’s right there. So no, I don’t think my face would suddenly feel acceptable just because nobody else saw it. It might be less embarrassing, maybe, but I’d still be bothered by it because I’d know it was there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would look normal to you if you could somehow completely remove or cover up the patch you're concerned about?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think if it was completely gone, or covered so well that I couldn’t see it at all, then maybe my face would look more normal to me. I think that’s the part I get stuck on — not my whole face exactly, but that one rough, sunken-looking patch. Once I see it, it’s like my eye just locks onto it.\n\nLike in the mirror, I’m not just seeing “my face.” I’m seeing that spot first. Same with photos. Even if it’s a group picture or something where other people probably wouldn’t even look that closely, I zoom right in on that side of my face, and it looks old to me, like worn down or beat-up compared to the other side. And people will say they barely notice anything, or that it just looks like normal skin texture, but that doesn’t really match what I see.\n\nSo if you mean if that exact patch was completely removed or hidden, then yeah, I think I’d probably feel like I looked more normal. But I don’t know… I also worry I’d still be checking to make sure it was really covered or really gone. But the main thing is that patch. That’s what makes my face look wrong to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check and fix your face if you were completely alone and there was no chance of anyone else seeing you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I probably would, at least some. Maybe not in exactly the same frantic way as when I’m about to be around people, but I don’t think it would just disappear because nobody could see me.\n\nLike, before work or before going over to my sister’s for game night, that’s when it gets the worst. I’ll be in the bathroom mirror way too long, turning my face one way and then the other, trying the overhead light, then leaning toward the side where the light hits different, checking if the patch looks deeper or rougher. And I’ll rub at it, even though I know that doesn’t really fix anything. It’s like I’m trying to smooth it out or make it look less obvious, but mostly I’m just making myself more focused on it.\n\nAnd then I start messing with how I’m going to look when I leave — like wearing my hat low, angling it a certain way, seeing if it shadows that side of my face. If I’m going to my sister’s and I think it looks too obvious, sometimes I’ll just come up with an excuse not to go. I’ll tell myself I’m tired or I’ve got something else, but really it’s because I got stuck looking at it and couldn’t stand the idea of walking in like that.\n\nSo if I was completely alone, no one around, no chance of anybody seeing me… I think I’d still check it because I’d still know it was there. But the fixing part, the hat, the trying to make it okay enough to go out — that’s definitely worse when I’m about to see people.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to check and fix your face is more about making yourself feel better about how you look or about avoiding what others might think of your appearance?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, honestly… but if I really slow down and think about it, the checking starts with me trying to feel okay with what I’m seeing. Like I’m trying to get to a point where I can look in the mirror and think, “Alright, it’s not that bad, I can leave the house.” But then it turns into thinking about other people seeing it, too.\n\nBefore work, for example, I’ll be in the bathroom and I’ll keep turning my face under different lights, trying to see if that patch looks rougher from one angle or more sunken from another. I’ll stand there way longer than I mean to. It’s not like I’m calmly grooming myself — it’s more like I’m trying to catch the worst version of it before somebody else does. And I’ll rub at the spot, like maybe I can smooth it out somehow, even though I know that doesn’t really work.\n\nSame thing if I’m supposed to go to my sister’s game night. I’ll check it, then check it again, then try wearing my hat low to see if it hides that side of my face better. And if I think it still looks too obvious, sometimes I just don’t go. I’ll make some excuse, like I’m tired or not feeling up to it, but really it’s because I couldn’t get myself comfortable with how my face looked.\n\nSo… I guess the fixing is partly to calm myself down, but it’s also to avoid walking into a place feeling like people are going to notice. It’s hard to separate the two, because if I don’t feel okay about it, I assume other people won’t either.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it starts more with the way it looks to me. Like, I’m the one in the bathroom mirror before work, turning my face this way and that way, trying the light from above, then from the side, checking if that patch looks rougher or more sunken. I’ll get stuck doing that longer than I mean to, and it’s like I’m trying to convince myself it looks okay enough.

But then it turns into thinking about other people, because if it looks bad to me, I assume it’s going to look bad to them too. Before I go to my sister’s game night, I’ll do the same thing — check it, rub at the spot like I can somehow smooth it out, then try putting my hat low and seeing if that hides it better. And if I still think it looks too obvious, sometimes I’ll just make an excuse not to go.

So… I guess the checking is mostly because I can’t stand how it looks to me, but the reason it gets so intense before work or seeing family is because I start worrying other people will notice it too.

It’s hard to explain without sounding ridiculous, but when I look in the mirror or especially in photos, I keep seeing this rough, sort of sunken-looking patch on one side of my face. Like that side looks older, beat-up, weathered… I don’t know, just wrong.\n\nOther people tell me they barely notice anything, or they say it’s not a big deal, but I can’t stop seeing it. It jumps out at me every time. I work construction, so I know I’ve been out in the sun and all that, but this feels like more than just normal aging to me when I look at it. It’s like my eye goes straight there, and then I can’t unsee it. That’s mostly why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Is this concern about your face affecting your daily life or causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it is. More than I’d like to admit, honestly. It’s not like I’m staying in bed all day or anything, I’m still going to work, but it’s starting to mess with normal things.\n\nLike in the mornings before a construction job, I’ll get caught checking my face in the mirror, then trying a different angle, different light, wiping at it, looking again… and then suddenly I’m running late getting out the door. I’ll tell myself, “Just leave, nobody’s thinking about this,” but then I’ll catch sight of that side of my face and it kind of hooks me.\n\nAnd at work, if someone’s taking site photos or group pictures, I’ve started dodging them. I’ll make some excuse like I need to grab a tool or I’ll stand off to the side, because I don’t want that patch showing up in a picture where everyone can look at it later. That part really bothers me.\n\nIt’s even spilled over at home too. My family does these game nights sometimes, and I’ve skipped a few because I just didn’t want to sit there under the lights with people looking at me. I know that sounds extreme for a face thing, but in the moment it feels like I can’t relax, like everyone’s going to notice it even if they say they don’t. So yeah… it’s causing distress, and it’s definitely getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions over and over, like touching or checking your face, in an attempt to make it look better or to reassure yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I don’t like admitting it, but yes. It’s become this whole routine, especially before I leave for work or if I’m supposed to go somewhere like my sister’s game night.\n\nI’ll be in the bathroom and I’ll check it in the mirror, then I’ll turn my face a little, then I’ll flip on a different light or lean closer, then back up, because somehow it looks different depending on where I’m standing. And I’ll keep thinking, “Okay, maybe it’s not that bad,” but then I catch it from another angle and it looks rough again, so I start over. Sometimes I rub at the spot, like maybe if I smooth it or wipe it enough it’ll look less noticeable, even though I know that doesn’t really make sense.\n\nAnd if I’m still feeling like it looks obvious, I’ll put on a hat and pull it kind of low, like that’s going to help hide that side of my face. Or I’ll just start thinking of excuses not to go. Like with my sister’s game night, I’ll tell myself I’m tired or I’ve got something to do early, but really it’s because I stood there looking at my face too long and convinced myself everyone’s going to see it.\n\nSo yeah, it’s checking, turning, rubbing, trying to cover it… and then checking again to see if any of that helped. It can eat up a lot more time than I mean for it to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry that something is wrong with your face beyond just its appearance, like it's dirty or contaminated in some way?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not like dirty or contaminated, if that’s what you mean. I mean, I work construction, so there are definitely days where I come home with dust or grime on me, and I’ll wash my face like anyone would. But this thing I’m worried about doesn’t feel like dirt, exactly.\n\nIt’s more like… when I look at it, it looks damaged or rough to me. Like the skin there is wrong-looking, not unclean. I don’t usually think, “Oh, there’s something on my face I need to scrub off,” except sometimes I rub at it because I’m hoping it’ll look smoother or less noticeable. But no, not dirty in that way.\n\nDoctor: Is it always this one particular patch on your face that bothers you, or are there other parts of your body that you also worry about?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly that one patch, yeah. I mean, I can get self-conscious like anybody, I guess, but the thing that really grabs me is this rough, kind of sunken-looking area on my face. It’s like one side of me looks older or more beat-up than the rest, if that makes sense.\n\nI notice it in the mirror, but photos are the worst. In a picture, I’ll zoom right to that side of my face and it’s like I can’t unsee it. Other people will say, “I barely notice anything,” or they’ll act like I’m making too much of it, but to me it stands out right away. It’s the reason I’m here, really — I can’t stop seeing it. Not really other body parts, no. It’s this spot on my face.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding social interactions or relationships because of your concerns about your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. More than I want to admit, honestly. It’s not like I’ve cut everybody off or anything, but I definitely avoid things now.\n\nLike with work, if they’re taking site photos or somebody wants a picture of the crew, I’ll kind of drift to the back or make an excuse like I need to grab something from the truck. I know that probably sounds ridiculous, because it’s just a job site and everyone’s dirty and tired anyway, but I start thinking that if the light hits my face wrong, that patch is going to show up really bad in the photo. And once it’s in a picture, I feel like it’s just there forever for people to look at.\n\nAnd getting out the door in the morning has gotten harder too. I’ve been late to construction jobs because I’m stuck in the bathroom checking my face, trying a hat, taking it off, looking again. Then I’m rushing and angry at myself because I know I should’ve left ten minutes ago.\n\nWith family stuff, yeah… I’ve skipped some of my sister’s game nights. I’ll say I’m tired from work, which is partly believable because I am tired, but the real reason is I don’t want to sit around a table with everyone facing me, looking at me under bright lights. I start imagining them noticing that side of my face, even if they don’t say anything. So I just don’t go sometimes. Then I feel bad afterward, because I like seeing them. The thing I can’t get past is that rough, kind of sunken-looking spot on my face.\n\nIt’s like when I look in the mirror, my eye goes right to it. It’s not really my whole body or a bunch of different features. It’s that spot on my face that keeps catching me.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your face affected your ability to perform your job as a construction worker?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. I’ve been late getting out the door for construction jobs because I get stuck checking it. I’ll be in the bathroom looking at that side of my face, then I’ll try a hat, change the angle, look again, maybe check near the window because the light is different there… and before I know it, I’m behind. I think that’s the part I get stuck on — not my whole face exactly, but that one rough, sunken-looking patch. Maybe not in exactly the same frantic way as when I’m about to be around people, but I don’t think it would just disappear because nobody could see me.\n\nLike, before work or before going over to my sister’s for game night, that’s when it gets the worst. Like I’m trying to get to a point where I can look in the mirror and think, “Alright, it’s not that bad, I can leave the house.” But then it turns into thinking about other people seeing it, too.\n\nBefore work, for example, I’ll be in the bathroom and I’ll keep turning my face under different lights, trying to see if that patch looks rougher from one angle or more sunken from another. Like with my sister’s game night, I’ll tell myself I’m tired or I’ve got something to do early, but really it’s because I stood there looking at my face too long and convinced myself everyone’s going to see it.\n\nSo yeah, it’s checking, turning, rubbing, trying to cover it… and then checking again to see if any of that helped. Not like dirty or contaminated, if that’s what you mean. I mean, I work construction, so there are definitely days where I come home with dust or grime on me, and I’ll wash my face like anyone would. But this thing I’m worried about doesn’t feel like dirt, exactly.\n\nIt’s more like… when I look at it, it looks damaged or rough to me. Like the skin there is wrong-looking, not unclean. I don’t usually think, “Oh, there’s something on my face I need to scrub off,” except sometimes I rub at it because I’m hoping it’ll look smoother or less noticeable. But I don’t have this fear that it’s contaminated or anything like that.\n\nI guess I just keep thinking it looks bad. Like weathered or sunken or off somehow. But no, not dirty in that way.\n\nDoctor: Is it always this one particular patch on your face that bothers you, or are there other parts of your body that you also worry about?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly that one patch, yeah. I mean, I can get self-conscious like anybody, I guess, but the thing that really grabs me is this rough, kind of sunken-looking area on my face. It’s like one side of me looks older or more beat-up than the rest, if that makes sense.\n\nI notice it in the mirror, but photos are the worst. In a picture, I’ll zoom right to that side of my face and it’s like I can’t unsee it. Other people will say, “I barely notice anything,” or they’ll act like I’m making too much of it, but to me it stands out right away. It’s the reason I’m here, really — I can’t stop seeing it. Not really other body parts, no. It’s this spot on my face.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding social interactions or relationships because of your concerns about your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. More than I want to admit, honestly. It’s not like I’ve cut everybody off or anything, but I definitely avoid things now.\n\nLike with work, if they’re taking site photos or somebody wants a picture of the crew, I’ll kind of drift to the back or make an excuse like I need to grab something from the truck. I know that probably sounds ridiculous, because it’s just a job site and everyone’s dirty and tired anyway, but I start thinking that if the light hits my face wrong, that patch is going to show up really bad in the photo. And once it’s in a picture, I feel like it’s just there forever for people to look at.\n\nAnd getting out the door in the morning has gotten harder too. I’ve been late to construction jobs because I’m stuck in the bathroom checking my face, trying a hat, taking it off, looking again. Then I’m rushing and angry at myself because I know I should’ve left ten minutes ago.\n\nWith family stuff, yeah… I’ve skipped some of my sister’s game nights. I’ll say I’m tired from work, which is partly believable because I am tired, but the real reason is I don’t want to sit around a table with everyone facing me, looking at me under bright lights. I start imagining them noticing that side of my face, even if they don’t say anything. So I just don’t go sometimes. Then I feel bad afterward, because I like seeing them. I just don’t want them looking at me.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about your face focused on this one particular patch, or do you sometimes worry about other aspects of your appearance as well?\n\nPatient: It’s really that one patch, mostly. I mean… I don’t love getting older in general, nobody does, but this isn’t like I’m standing there picking apart every single thing about myself. The thing I can’t get past is that rough, kind of sunken-looking spot on my face.\n\nIt’s like when I look in the mirror, my eye goes right to it. And in photos it’s worse — I’ll look at the picture and it’s like one side of my face looks old, worn down, beat-up, and I can’t see the rest of the picture normally after that. Other people keep telling me they barely notice anything, or they say it’s just lighting or normal skin, but to me it stands out immediately.\n\nSo, yeah, I’m here because I can’t stop seeing that one area. It’s not really my whole body or a bunch of different features. It’s that spot on my face that keeps catching me.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your face affected your ability to perform your job as a construction worker?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. I mean, I still do the work once I’m there — I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t swing a hammer or carry materials because of my face — but it’s affected getting there and how I act on site.\n\nThe biggest thing is the morning. I’ve been late getting out the door for construction jobs because I get stuck checking it. I’ll be in the bathroom looking at that side of my face, then I’ll try a hat, change the angle, look again, maybe check near the window because the light is different there… and before I know it, I’m behind. Then I’m driving in all stressed out and mad at myself because it’s not like the crew cares about my face, they care that I’m there on time.\n\nAnd on the job site, if someone’s taking photos — progress pictures, crew pictures, whatever — I try to dodge them. I’ll step away or act like I need to grab something. I just don’t want that patch showing up in a picture where everyone can see it later. So, yeah, it hasn’t stopped me from doing construction, exactly, but it has definitely gotten in the way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to check your face, or is it something you can stop doing if you want to?\n\nPatient: It feels like I have to, yeah. I mean, I can tell myself, “Just leave it alone, you’re going to be late,” but then I’ll still go back to the bathroom mirror one more time. And then one more time after that.\n\nBefore work especially, I’ll stand there turning my face different ways, like trying to catch it under the overhead light, then maybe by the window, because somehow I think if I can see exactly how bad it looks, I’ll feel better. But I usually don’t. I’ll rub at the spot too, like maybe it’ll smooth out or look less rough, even though I know that probably doesn’t make sense.\n\nSame thing before going to my sister’s game night. I’ll check it and check it, and then I’ll try wearing a hat low, look again, adjust it, take it off, put it back on. If I decide it looks too obvious that day, sometimes I’ll just make an excuse not to go. So… I guess I can stop eventually, because I do leave the bathroom at some point, but it doesn’t feel like I can just choose to not check it. It kind of pulls me back.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if you could somehow fix or hide this patch on your face, your concerns and behaviors related to it would completely go away?\n\nPatient: I want to say yes, because it feels like everything is about that one spot. Like if that rough, kind of sunken-looking patch just wasn’t there, or if I could cover it so it didn’t show in the mirror or in photos, then I think I’d finally be able to stop staring at it.\n\nBecause that’s really what brings me here — I can’t stop seeing it. I’ll look at my face and it’s like my eye goes straight to that area, like one side of me looks older, worn down, beat-up almost. And then people tell me, “I barely notice it,” or “It’s just the lighting,” but that doesn’t really settle it for me, because I still see it immediately.\n\nSo… yeah, I think if it were truly fixed or hidden, the worry would probably go way down. Maybe completely. It’s hard to imagine because right now it’s like I can’t look at myself normally without that patch jumping out at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control when you're checking your face, or do you feel like you're doing it automatically?\n\nPatient: It’s kind of both, but more like it’s automatic once I get started. Like before work, I’ll tell myself I’m just going to take one quick look in the bathroom mirror, make sure it’s not too bad, and then leave. But then I’m turning my face this way and that way under the light, then I’ll move closer, then farther away, and I’ll check it again like maybe I missed something.\n\nAnd the lighting makes it worse, because I’ll think, “Well, it looks bad under the bathroom light, but what about by the window?” So I go over there and check it in that light too. Then I’ll rub at the spot, like I’m trying to smooth it down or see if it feels as bad as it looks, even though I know standing there doing that isn’t really helping anything.\n\nIt doesn’t feel like I sit there and calmly decide to do all that. It’s more like I get pulled into it. Same thing if I’m going to my sister’s game night — I’ll check it, put a hat on low, look again, adjust the hat, take it off, put it back on, and then sometimes if I think it’s too obvious, I’ll just make an excuse not to go. So yeah… I don’t feel very in control of it. It’s like I’m doing it before I even fully realize how long I’ve been standing there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are judging you because of this patch on your face?\n\nPatient: I guess… yeah, I worry they are. I don’t know if I can prove it, because people tell me they barely notice anything, and maybe they mean that. But when I see it in the mirror or in photos, that rough, kind of sunken-looking patch is the first thing I see. It makes one side of my face look old, like worn down or beat-up, and then I assume other people must be seeing the same thing.\n\nSo if someone looks at me a little too long, or if I’m talking to somebody face-to-face, I start thinking, “They’re looking at that spot.” Even if they don’t say anything. It’s hard to believe they’re not judging it when it looks so obvious to me. That’s pretty much why I’m here, honestly — I can’t stop seeing it, even when everyone else says it’s nothing or they barely notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that if nobody else could see this patch on your face, you would still feel the need to check on it and try to fix it?\n\nPatient: I think… yeah, I probably still would. I mean, part of me wants to say if nobody else could see it, then maybe I’d relax, because a lot of the panic is about going out and having someone notice it. But even when I’m alone, it’s still there for me. I still see it.\n\nLike before work, even if I’m not really seeing anyone close up right away, I’ll get stuck in the bathroom checking it. I turn my face under the light, then I’ll move a little, check from another angle, sometimes go by a different light because I’m trying to figure out if it looks worse there. And I rub at it, like somehow I’m going to smooth it out or make it look less rough, even though I know that doesn’t really do anything.\n\nSame thing if I’m supposed to go to my sister’s game night. I’ll check it, put a hat on low, look again, adjust it, look again… and if I think that patch is too obvious, I might just make some excuse and not go. So even if nobody else could see it, I think I’d still be checking, because I would know it was there. It’s like I can’t leave it alone once I notice it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your life would be significantly different if you didn't have this concern about the patch on your face?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it would be different. Pretty noticeably, honestly. I don’t know if everything in my life would magically be perfect, but this one thing eats up way more space than it should.\n\nLike with work, I do construction, so mornings already aren’t exactly relaxed. I need to get out the door, get to the site, be ready to go. But I’ve been late because I get stuck checking my face before I leave. I’ll think I’m just checking once, and then it turns into looking at it under one light, then another, messing with my hat, trying to figure out if it’s as bad as I think it is. And then suddenly I’m behind, and I’m rushing, or I’m showing up later than I should. That’s embarrassing too, because it’s not like I want to tell the guys, “Sorry, I was staring at my face in the mirror again.”\n\nAnd then there are little things that shouldn’t be a big deal, like site photos. If someone’s taking pictures of the crew or progress shots and people are in them, I try to dodge out of the way. I don’t want my face showing up in some picture where everyone can zoom in or where I’m caught at a bad angle. I know that probably sounds excessive, but in the moment it feels like I have to avoid it.\n\nFamily stuff too. My sister has game nights sometimes, and I’ve skipped some of those because I couldn’t get myself to go if I thought the patch looked too obvious that day. I’ll make an excuse, like I’m tired or something came up, but really it’s because I don’t want to sit across from people with them looking at my face all night.\n\nSo yeah… if I didn’t have this concern, I think I’d be more on time, I’d probably show up for things more, and I wouldn’t be spending so much energy trying to hide or avoid being looked at.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would be acceptable to you if you could be certain that nobody else noticed this patch?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… or at least I don’t think I’d be completely okay with it.\n\nI mean, if I could be absolutely sure nobody else noticed, maybe that would take some of the pressure off. I wouldn’t be sitting there thinking every person is staring at it or judging me for it. But the thing is, I still notice it. I’m here because I can’t stop seeing it myself. In the mirror, in photos, it’s like my eye goes straight to that rough, sunken-looking patch, and then the rest of my face kind of disappears.\n\nIt makes one side of my face look old to me, like beat-up or worn down, and even if people keep saying they barely notice anything, I don’t really experience it that way. To me it’s right there. So no, I don’t think my face would suddenly feel acceptable just because nobody else saw it. It might be less embarrassing, maybe, but I’d still be bothered by it because I’d know it was there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face would look normal to you if you could somehow completely remove or cover up the patch you're concerned about?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think if it was completely gone, or covered so well that I couldn’t see it at all, then maybe my face would look more normal to me. I think that’s the part I get stuck on — not my whole face exactly, but that one rough, sunken-looking patch. Once I see it, it’s like my eye just locks onto it.\n\nLike in the mirror, I’m not just seeing “my face.” I’m seeing that spot first. Same with photos. Even if it’s a group picture or something where other people probably wouldn’t even look that closely, I zoom right in on that side of my face, and it looks old to me, like worn down or beat-up compared to the other side. And people will say they barely notice anything, or that it just looks like normal skin texture, but that doesn’t really match what I see.\n\nSo if you mean if that exact patch was completely removed or hidden, then yeah, I think I’d probably feel like I looked more normal. But I don’t know… I also worry I’d still be checking to make sure it was really covered or really gone. But the main thing is that patch. That’s what makes my face look wrong to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel the need to check and fix your face if you were completely alone and there was no chance of anyone else seeing you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I probably would, at least some. Maybe not in exactly the same frantic way as when I’m about to be around people, but I don’t think it would just disappear because nobody could see me.\n\nLike, before work or before going over to my sister’s for game night, that’s when it gets the worst. I’ll be in the bathroom mirror way too long, turning my face one way and then the other, trying the overhead light, then leaning toward the side where the light hits different, checking if the patch looks deeper or rougher. And I’ll rub at it, even though I know that doesn’t really fix anything. It’s like I’m trying to smooth it out or make it look less obvious, but mostly I’m just making myself more focused on it.\n\nAnd then I start messing with how I’m going to look when I leave — like wearing my hat low, angling it a certain way, seeing if it shadows that side of my face. If I’m going to my sister’s and I think it looks too obvious, sometimes I’ll just come up with an excuse not to go. I’ll tell myself I’m tired or I’ve got something else, but really it’s because I got stuck looking at it and couldn’t stand the idea of walking in like that.\n\nSo if I was completely alone, no one around, no chance of anybody seeing me… I think I’d still check it because I’d still know it was there. But the fixing part, the hat, the trying to make it okay enough to go out — that’s definitely worse when I’m about to see people.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to check and fix your face is more about making yourself feel better about how you look or about avoiding what others might think of your appearance?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, honestly… but if I really slow down and think about it, the checking starts with me trying to feel okay with what I’m seeing. Like I’m trying to get to a point where I can look in the mirror and think, “Alright, it’s not that bad, I can leave the house.” But then it turns into thinking about other people seeing it, too.\n\nBefore work, for example, I’ll be in the bathroom and I’ll keep turning my face under different lights, trying to see if that patch looks rougher from one angle or more sunken from another. I’ll stand there way longer than I mean to. It’s not like I’m calmly grooming myself — it’s more like I’m trying to catch the worst version of it before somebody else does. And I’ll rub at the spot, like maybe I can smooth it out somehow, even though I know that doesn’t really work.\n\nSame thing if I’m supposed to go to my sister’s game night. I’ll check it, then check it again, then try wearing my hat low to see if it hides that side of my face better. And if I think it still looks too obvious, sometimes I just don’t go. I’ll make some excuse, like I’m tired or not feeling up to it, but really it’s because I couldn’t get myself comfortable with how my face looked.\n\nSo… I guess the fixing is partly to calm myself down, but it’s also to avoid walking into a place feeling like people are going to notice. It’s hard to separate the two, because if I don’t feel okay about it, I assume other people won’t either.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your need to check your face is more about the way it looks to you or about what you think others might think of it?\n\nPatient: I think it starts more with the way it looks to me. Like, I’m the one in the bathroom mirror before work, turning my face this way and that way, trying the light from above, then from the side, checking if that patch looks rougher or more sunken. I’ll get stuck doing that longer than I mean to, and it’s like I’m trying to convince myself it looks okay enough.\n\nBut then it turns into thinking about other people, because if it looks bad to me, I assume it’s going to look bad to them too. Before I go to my sister’s game night, I’ll do the same thing — check it, rub at the spot like I can somehow smooth it out, then try putting my hat low and seeing if that hides it better. And if I still think it looks too obvious, sometimes I’ll just make an excuse not to go.\n\nSo… I guess the checking is mostly because I can’t stand how it looks to me, but the reason it gets so intense before work or seeing family is because I start worrying other people will notice it too."
